12 allatu 16vatu nāḷil kātu kuttappaṭukiṟatu. perumpālum itu 6vatu allatu 8vatu mātattil naṭaipeṟukiṟatu. 6ām allatu 8ām mātattil cōṟu tīṟṟuc caṭaṅku naṭaipeṟukiṟatu. kiriyai muṭivil kuḻantaikkuc ciṟṟāṭaiyuṭutti kuḻantaiyaik kōyilukku eṭuttuc ceṉṟu ciṟṟāṭai kōyilukkuk koṭukkappaṭukiṟatu. mutalāvatu allatu mūṉṟāvatu vārattil muṭi iṟakkappaṭukiṟatu. ucciyil allatu neṟṟikku mēl oru piṭi mayir viṭṭu nāvitaṉ talaiyai maḻittu viṭuvāṉ. cila pakutikaḷil uṟumālkaṭṭu eṉṉum viruntuk koṇṭāṭṭam naṭakkum varaiyil iḷaiñar talaiyai maḻittuk koḷḷamāṭṭārkaḷ. ciṟuvar, ciṟumiyaraip paḷḷikku vaittal ēṭu toṭakkutal eṉappaṭukiṟatu. paḷḷikkūṭa āciriyar mūṉṟu paṉai ōlaic caṭṭaṅkaḷil arivariyai eḻuti eḻuttukkaḷukku mañcaḷ pūcit tūpaṅ kāṭṭik koṭuppatu ik kiriyaiyākum. ciṟuvar paḷḷikkuc cellum vayatu 5. peṇ paruvamaṭaintatum cāmarttiyak kaliyāṇam naṭaipeṟukiṟatu. pūppu aṭaintatum avaḷ vīṭṭil maṟaivāṉa oru iṭattil ēḻu allatu 11 nāṭkaḷukku viṭappaṭukiṟāḷ. piṉpu avaḷukku muḻukkāṭṭappaṭukiṟatu. appoḻutu naṇparum cuṟṟattavarum aḻaikkappaṭuvārkaḷ. muḻukkāṭṭum pōtu peṇkaḷ oruvar mītoruvar mañcaḷ nīr teḷittuk koḷvar. piṉ peṇṇukku ārātti kāṭṭuvārkaḷ. cila pakutikaḷil mūṉṟāvatu nāḷ kaliyāṇa māṉa peṇkaḷ kūṭiyiruntu cila kiriyaikaḷiṉ piṉ peṇṇaic cuṟṟi niṉṟu kaitaṭṭi āṭuvārkaḷ. (iv vaḻakkaṅkaḷ ippoḻutu perum pālum maṟaintuviṭṭaṉa.)
tirumaṇam
tirumaṇap pēccu maṇamakaṉiṉ tantaiyāl toṭaṅkappaṭukiṟatu. toṭaṅkumuṉ avaṉ iru pakutiyāriṉ cātakaṅkaḷaiyum cōtiṭar mūlam pārttup poruttamirukkiṟatā eṉṟu aṟintu koḷvāṉ. kaliyāṇam niccayamāṉatum maṇamakaṉiṉ tantai, muṉṉāl mēḷam aṭittuc cella cila naṇparkaḷuṭaṉ 7 allatu 9 vāḻaik kulaika ḷuṭaṉum mañcaḷ pūciya tēṅkāykaḷōṭum peṇṇiṉ tantai vīṭṭukkuc cītaṉam niccayippataṟkuc celvāṉ. avarkaḷ oru nipantaṉai allatu paṭṭōlai eḻutikkoṇṭu kaliyāṇattukku nāḷ vaip pārkaḷ. iru pakutiyār vīṭṭu muṟṟaṅkaḷilum pantaliṭappaṭum. ettaṉai pantaṟkālkaḷ naṭavēṇṭumeṉpatil cila camayaṅkaḷil tarkkam eḻuvatuṇṭu. mutaṟ pantaṟkāl vaṭakiḻakkut ticaiyil naṭap paṭum. itu otiya marakkālāka virukkum. naṭummuṉ ataṟkuc cantaṉam, mañcaḷ, kuṅkumam mutaliyaṉa pūci māvilai, taruppaippul mutaliyaṉa kaṭṭappaṭum. naṭuvataṉ muṉ paruttikkoṭṭai kalanta pāl vārttu tēṅkāyuṭaikkappaṭum. pantal veḷḷai kaṭṭi pākkuk kulaikaḷ, teṉṉam pū, ilaikaḷ, kaṇṇāṭi viḷakkukaḷ, kākitap pūkkaḷ, vattit tāḷkaḷāl alaṅkarikkappaṭum. pantaliṉ muṉ vil vaṭivākak kampukaḷ kaṭṭit tōraṇam tūkki vāḻaikaḷ naṭṭu alaṅkarikkappaṭum. pantaliṉ mattiyil kaliyāṇak kāl nāṭṭappaṭṭirukkum. iḵtu aracāṇikkāl eṉappaṭum. ataṉ pakkattil kiṇṭappaṭṭa ōmakuṇṭam cāṇiyāl meḻukappaṭṭirukkum. itaṉ pakkattil viḷakkukaḷ koḷutti vaikkappaṭṭirukkum. avaṟṟiṉ pakkattil kaḷimaṇṇāl ceyta yāṉaiyiṉ mutukil mūṉṟu allatu ēḻu, niṟam pūciya pāṉaikaḷ oṉṟiṉ mēloṉṟāka aṭukki vaikkappaṭṭirukkum. mēlē irukkum pāṉaiyiṉ vāyil mañcaḷ pūciya tēṅkāy vaikkappaṭum. maṇamakaṉ pakutiyār maṇamakaḷ vīṭṭukkut tāli kūṟaikaḷuṭaṉ celvārkaḷ. celvar pallakkil allatu kutirai mēlēṟip pōvar. vaṇṇāṉ vītiyil nila pāvāṭai virittuc celvāṉ. vēṟu iruvar cāmarai vīcuvar. nālvar mēṟkaṭṭi (mēlāppu) piṭippar. vāttiyakārar vāttiya molippar. iru nāvitar caṅku ūtuvar. nāṭṭiyappeṇkaḷ nāṭṭiya māṭic celvar. pala iḷaiñar tīvaṭṭi, veḷḷaik kuṭai, koṭi, virutu mutaliyaṉa piṭittuc celvar. maṇamakaṉ vantatum maṇamāṉa peṇkaḷ ārātti kāṭṭuvar. peṇṇiṉ tāy allatu cakōtari oru kiṇṇattil picainta vāḻaippaḻattaiyum. pālaiyum pantaliluḷḷa pīṭattil vaikkumpaṭi koṭuppar. vīṭṭil maṟaivāka irunta peṇ ippoḻutu pallakkil kōyilukku eṭuttuc ceṉṟapiṉ pantalukku koṇṭu varappaṭuvāḷ. iruvarum kiḻakku nōkkiyirukkumpaṭi aṅkuḷḷa pīṭattil iruttappaṭuvārkaḷ. purōkitar peṇṇiṉ iṭatu kaiyilum maṇamakaṉiṉ valatu kaiyilum kaṅkaṇaṅ kaṭṭiyapiṉ kiriyaikaḷaip purivar. tāliyai maṇamakaṉiṉ cakōtari eṭuttup purōkitariṭam koṭuppāḷ. avar ataṟkut tūpaṅ kāṭṭiyapiṉ capaiyōriṭattil koṭuppār. avarkaḷ ovvoruvarāka atait toṭuvārkaḷ. piṉpu maṇamakaṉ tāli kaṭṭuvāṉ. piṉṉāl niṟkum avaṉuṭaiya cakōtari ataṉai naṉṟākak kaṭṭiviṭuvāḷ. piṉpu av viṭattiṟ kūṭiyirukkum kaliyāṇamāṉa peṇkaḷ nellum veṟṟilai yum vaitta nāḻiyāl maṇamakaṉ, maṇamakaḷ eṉṉum iruvariṉ talaiyaiyum cuṟṟuvārkaḷ. appoḻutu purōkitar tēṅkā yuṭaippār. piṉ aruntati kāṭṭutal, ammi mitittal mutaliya kiriyaikaḷ naṭaipeṟum.
maraṇam
oruvaṉukku maraṇam aṇukumpōtu purōkitar aḻaikkap paṭukiṟār. avar ciṟitu pañca kauviyattai avaṉ vāyiṉuḷ viṭuvār. piṉpu nōyāḷi pacumāṭṭiṉ vālaip piṭittup purōkitarukku ataṉait tāṉamākak koṭuppāṉ. maraṇamaṭaintatum talai vaṭakkē kiṭakkumpaṭi pirētam kiṭattappaṭukiṟatu. pakkattil niṟpavarkaḷ ‘civā’ eṉṟu mūṉṟu muṟai pirētattiṉ kātil colli neṟṟikkum, neñcukkum tirunīṟu pūcuvar. maraṇac ceyti caṅku ūti ayalavarkaḷukku aṟivikkappaṭum. peṇkaḷ cavattaic cuṟṟiyiruntu oppārivait taḻuvar. muṟṟattil nālu kampukaḷ naṭṭu veḷḷaikaṭṭip pantaliṭap paṭum. aṅku piṇam eḷḷeṇṇaiyum, arappum talaiyil vaittu muḻukkāṭṭiyapiṉ pantaliṉ mattiyil kiṭattappaṭum. piṉpu piṇam, pāṭai allatu taṇṭikaiyil vaittut talai muṉpuṟam niṟkat takkatākac cuṭukāṭṭukku eṭuttuc cellappaṭum. cellum vaḻiyil aricippori, paruttivitai, atti ilai, kācu eṉpaṉa eṟiyappaṭum. piṇattai viṟakiṉ mītu kiṭattiyapiṉ vāykkarici pōṭṭuk koḷḷik kuṭamuṭaittut tī mūṭṭappaṭum. īmac caṭaṅkukkuc camukamāyiruntōr ellōrum nīrāṭuvar.
iṟantavaṉiṉ maṉaivi kaimpeṇ eṉappaṭuvāḷ. ivaḷuṭaiya tāli mūṉṟāvatu nāḷ kaḻaṟṟappaṭum.
puttaḷak karaiyār
puttaḷak karaiyārkaḷiṭaiyil mūṉṟu vakaik kaliyāṇaṅkaḷ naṭaipeṟukiṉṟaṉa. oṉṟu mālai maṇam. maṇamakaṉiṉ cakōtari peṇṇaip pūmālai vācaṉaip poruḷkaḷālalaṅkaritta piṉ avaḷ kaḻuttil tāliyaṇivāḷ. kaliyāṇam ceytukoṇṭa iruvarukkum orumittu vāḻap piriyamillāviṭil avarkaḷ pirintu koḷḷalām. pirintukoḷḷa virumpum pakuti kiṭṭa uḷḷa kōyilukku 25 iṟacāl koṭukka vēṇṭum.
iraṇṭāvatu maṇam ciṟutāli. itu yātum kiriyaikaḷillāmal muṉ kūṟiyatu pōlat tāli tarittal. kaliyāṇa nīkkam ceytu koḷḷa virumpum pakuti 25 iṟacāl āṟupaṇam kōyilukkut taṇṭam koṭukka vēṇṭum.
mūṉṟāvatu mañcaḷ pūcal. itu tāli kaṭṭāmal maṇañ ceytu koḷvatu. maṇamakaṉiṉ cakōtari peṇṇiṉ uṭaiyil araitta mañcaḷaip pūci viṭuvāḷ. kaliyāṇa nīkkam ceytu koḷḷum pakuti āṟu iṟacāl mūṉṟu paṇam taṇṭamākak kaṭṭavēṇṭum.
aṇivakai
āṭavar aṇipavai
oṭṭu - irattiṉam vaikkappaṭṭa poṉ vaḷaiyam. kātil aṇiyappaṭuvatu.
kaṭukkaṉ - kātil aṇiyappaṭuvatu. itil pala vakaikaḷuṇṭu.
ceṭṭik kaṭukkaṉ - 10 mutal 11 aṅkulac cuṟṟaḷavuḷḷatu. poṉ kampikaḷai muṟukkic ceyyappaṭuvatu. naṭuvil irattiṉam vaikkappaṭ ṭirukkum. koḻumpuc ceṭṭikaḷ itil aintu allatu āṟu vaḷaiyaṅ kaḷai ovvoru kātilumaṇivar.
cūṭṭuk kaṭukkaṉ - nīṇṭa vaṭṭamāṉa vaḷaiyam.
arippuccārik kaṭukkaṉ - poṉ pūvarumpukaḷ kōkkappaṭṭa poṉ kampi.
uruttirāṭcak kaṭukkaṉ - uruttirākkam naṭuvil vaikkappaṭṭa kaṭukkaṉ.
pulimukak kaṭukkaṉ - muṉpakkattil pulimukam poṟikkappaṭṭa kaṭukkaṉ.
muruku - kātiṉ mēṟpakutiyil aṇiyappaṭuvatu; kīḻē muttu allatu irattiṉakkal toṅkaviṭappaṭṭiruppatu.
carappaḷḷi - poṉ caṅkili.
patakkam - caṅkiliyil kōttuk kaḻuttilaṇiyappaṭuvatu.
araiñāṉ - poṉ allatu veḷḷiyāṟ ceytu araiyilaṇiyappaṭuvatu.
miñci - kāṟperuviralilaṇiyum veḷḷi mōtiram.
kāṟai - itu naṭuvil cantiraṉiṉ vaṭivam toṅkumpaṭiyāka veḷḷi allatu poṉṉāṟ ceyyappaṭṭa mālai.
peṇkaḷaṇikaḷ
kuppi - koṇṭaiyil cerukappaṭuvatu; kaṭukkāyaḷavu parumai yuṭaiyatāka irattiṉak kaṟkaḷ patikkappaṭṭatu.
cuṭṭi - mūkkukku nērē neṟṟiyil toṅkumpaṭi talaiyil aṇiyappaṭuvatu.
ciṇukki - caṅkili kōtta taṭṭaiyāṉa poṉ takaṭu; kātaṇiyil māṭṭi koṇṭaiyil koḷuvappaṭuvatu.
piṟaipoḻutu - piṟaiyaiyum cūriyaṉaiyum pōṟ ceyyap paṭṭa iraṇṭu poṉṉāparaṇaṅkaḷ; oṉṟai oṉṟu pārkkumpaṭi talaiyi laṇiyappaṭuvaṉa.
paṭṭam - neṟṟiyil kaṭṭum poṉ takaṭu.
mūkkutti - mūkkiṉ iṭappakkatti laṇiyappaṭuvatu; muttut toṅkaviṭappaṭṭiruppatu.
nattu - mūkkiṉ iraṇṭu tuvāraṅkaḷukku naṭuvil aṇiyap paṭuvatu; muttu allatu irattiṉakkal toṅkaviṭappaṭṭiruppatu.
tōṭu - pūviṉ vaṭiviṉatākak kātilaṇiyappaṭuvatu.
tulākku - muttut toṅkaviṭappaṭṭa tōṭu.
koppu - mēṟkātilaṇiyappaṭum taṅka vaḷaiyam.
nākapaṭam - koppukkuk kīḻ aṇiyappaṭuvatu. pāmpiṉ talaiyaip pōl tōṟṟamaḷippatu.
maṇi - pala paṭṭukkaḷil aṇiyappaṭum maṇi kōtta mālai.
cavaṭi - tōḷil poṟuttu niṟkumpaṭi aṇiyappaṭum mālai.
aṭṭikai - irattiṉak kaṟkaḷ patitta kaḻuttaṇi. kaḻuttil oṭṭakkaṭṭappaṭuvatu.
uḷkaṭṭu - kalpatikkāta aṭṭikai.
tāli - kaliyāṇattiṉpōtu kaṇavaṉ poṉ kayiṟṟil allatu mañcaṭ kayiṟṟil kōttu maṉaivi kaḻuttil aṇivatu.
nelli - iraṇṭu poṉ allatu veḷḷik kampikaḷai muṟukkic ceytatu; muḻaṅkaikku mēl aṇiyappaṭuvatu.
kai vaḷaiyalkaḷ - itil kāppu, kaṅkaṇam, vaḷaiyal, cāri. kaṭakam eṉap pala vakaikaḷuṇṭu. ivai veḷḷi allatu poṉṉi ṉāl ceyyappaṭṭu maṇikkaṭṭil aṇiyappaṭuvaṉa. ivai uḷḷē kuḻal uṭaiyaṉavāy oru aṅkulak kuṟukkaḷavuṭaiyaṉavākavirukkum.
oṭṭiyāṇam - poṉ allatu veḷḷiyiṉāṟ ceyta araip paṭṭikai.
taṇṭai - uḷḷē paraliṭappaṭṭa kāppu; veḷḷiyāṟ ceyyappaṭuvatu. kālilaṇiyappaṭuvatu.
cataṅkai - itu taṇṭaikkuk kīḻ aṇiyappaṭum pātacaram.
pāṭakam - kutiraik kaṭivāḷattiṉ mēṟpākam pōṉṟa periya veḷḷic caṅkili. itu kaṇaikkālukku mēl aṇiyappaṭuvatu.
kolucu - muṉ kūṟappaṭṭatu pōṉṟatu.
kāl mōtiraṅkaḷ - ivaṟṟil palavakaikaḷuṇṭu. nakamūṭi, pīli, muṉtāṅki, mayilaṭi, makaramīṉ.
mōtiram - irupālārum irattiṉakkaṟkaḷ patitta veḷḷi allatu taṅka mōtiraṅkaḷ palavaṟṟaik kaiyilaṇivar.
koḻumpuc ceṭṭip peṇkaḷ peṉiccai eṉṉum poṉ koṇṭai ūcikaḷaik koṇṭaiyil kuttik koḷvar. mēṟpakkam poṉ patikkappaṭṭu irattiṉak kaṟkaḷ patikkappaṭṭa ōṭṭu cīppaik koṇṭaiyilaṇivar. avarkaḷ mēṟkātil kappu eṉṉum aṇiyai yum ataṟkuk kīḻ kirāpu eṉṉum aṇikaḷaiyum, urukkumaṇi eṉṉum aintu allatu āṟu vaḷaiyaṅkaḷaiyum avaṟṟiṉ kīḻ cavaṭik kaṭukkaṉ eṉṉum tōḷvarai toṅkum mūṉṟu periya āparaṇaṅkaḷaiyum aṇivar. avarkaḷ kaḻuttu, kai, pātam eṉpavaṟṟil pala āparaṇaṅ kaḷai aṇivar.

1. It is admitted both by Indian and European scholars that the civilization of the Tamil Nation was, in the main, due to the Aryan Colonists in the South-Tamil studies p.233 - M.Srinivasa Iyangar.M.A., (1914).
itu paḻaiya ārāycci. ik karuttuṭaiyavarkaḷē perumpālum tamiḻar varalāṟṟiṉai eḻuta muyaṉṟu vantārkaḷ.
“When the Aryans entered India they had no script of their own, and their script was the script of their enemies, the Dasus (Dravidians)” - Fr. Heras.
From prehistoric times these Dravids have been the most intellectual, civilized, cultured people of India, famed for arts and architecture, piety and religions with elaborate rites and symbolisms. The last they have left to us in magnificent neolithic cave temples, carved out of rocky mountains, and in the most intractable stone beautiful design, elaborate in rich decoration with grandeur and yet delicacy. The artists are quite unsurpassed by any race in the world. The people are deep thinkers powerful rulers and administrators who never permitted any Aryan interference in Central or Southern India, in its government, religion or occupations, though their upper classes affected a good 
deal of Sanskrit - A short study in the science of comparative religions p. 175
- J.G.R. Forlong.
These last (Early Vedic Hymns) are full of faded decayed, and quite unintelligible words, and forms, and in some points nearer Greek then ordinary Sanskrit - (Maxmuller). So that the Aryan tongue first appeared in India, was not intelligibly put into words of forms. This was a work of several centuries later when civilized Dravidian Pandits wrote out for Aryans in their sacred Devanagari or Dravid character. Necessarily they thought the illiterate colonists how to write their language and reform their faith and mythology. The same process was then going on in the west when Greeks were beginning to write their Aryan tongue the Semito Phoenician characters aided by Egyptians and Syrians they too were organizing an Aryan mythology and faith 
- Ibeid p, 247.
itu naṭunilaimaiyōṭu ceyyappaṭṭa mēṟṟicai aṟiñariṉ ārāyccik karuttu.
1. paḻaṅkaṟkālam: makkaḷ muraṭāṉa kallāyutaṅkaḷaic ceytu payaṉpaṭuttiya kālam.
2. putiya kaṟkālam: makkaḷ aḻuttamāṉa kallāyutaṅkaḷaic ceytu payaṉpaṭuttiya kālam.
3. mēṟku āciya makkaḷum tamiḻ makkaḷum orē makkaṭ kulattaic cērntavarkaḷākak kāṇappaṭṭamaiyālum, iru makkaḷiṉ paṇṭai nākarikamum peritum ottiruntamai
yālum, mēṟku āciya makkaḷē intiya nāṭṭai aṭaintavarkaḷāvar, eṉa ārāycci yāḷar karutuvārāyiṉar. iṉṟaiya ārāycciyil mēṟku āciya makkaḷē intiya nāṭṭiṉiṉṟum mēṟku āciya nāṭukaḷiṟ ceṉṟu kuṭiyēṟiyavarkaḷāvar eṉat teḷi vuṟukiṉṟatu. cōḻa nāṭṭiṉiṉṟum ceṉṟu taikiras, yūpirātas āṟṟiṉ vaṭapāl kuṭiyēṟiya makkaḷ tam nāṭṭukkuc cāltiyā eṉap peyariṭṭārkaḷ eṉṟum, ap peyar cōḻatēcam eṉpataṉ tiripākum eṉṟum ē.ci. tās eṉpār tamatu irukku vēta intiyā eṉṉum nūlil naṉku viḷakkip pōntār.
“cintup piratēcattiliruntu namatu nākarikam pāpilōṉiyā ekiptu mutaliya tēcaṅkaḷukkup paraviyatu. avviṭaṅkaḷiliruntu intiyāviṟkup paraviyatu eṉṟa airōppiya ārāycciyāḷariṉ eṇṇam tavaṟeṉṟē karutavēṇṭum. pāpilōṉiyā vil ki.mu. 700il oru tamiḻk kuṭittaṉam iruntatu. teṉṉāṭṭiliruntu tamiḻ nākarikam urōmāpurikkum kirēkka nāṭṭiṟkum paraviyatu. iṉṉum takkiṇa pīṭa pūmiyaic cērnta vēṭṭuvar eṉṟa kuṟiñci nila makkaḷ yavaṉa tēcattiṟkuc ceṉṟu taṅkaḷ aracai nilainiṟuttit taṅkaḷ peyaraiyum an nāṭṭiṟkuk koṭuttārkaḷ. ituvē kirīṭ (Crete) tēcam. itaṉ nākarikattiṟkum tamiḻ nākarikattiṟkum oppumai mikutiyāyuḷḷatu. maṇimalarttiraṭṭu - tiru.vi.ār. irāmaccantira tīṭcitar em.ē. - varalāṟṟu ārāycciyāḷar palkalaikkaḻakam, ceṉṉai.
The Development of Mohenjo-Daro script, the religion of these two countries and that of Egypt, the titles of kings, the number of zodiacal constellations among the Proto-Indian People and the relative position of these constellations, the changing of the Proto-Indian constellation of the Harp (yal) for Turus (the bull) which might have taken place in Sumar, the tradition of the ancient people of Mesopotamia recorded by Berosus, the parallel by Bible account of Gen. 11 : 1 - 5.
All point to the same conclusion, that the migration of the Mediterranean race commenced from India and extended through south Mesopotamia and North Africa spread through Crete Cyprus, Greece Italy and Spain and across the Prynees reached central Europe and the British Isles. The route starting from Ceylon to Ireland is marked by uninterupted chain of dolmen and other megaliths that seem to be the relics of the enterprising and highly civilized race which is termed the Mediterranean by the anthropologists and which in India, has been quite unreasonably despised under the name Dravidian - Fr. Heras.
1. pāli moḻi, ilaṅkaiyil makkaḷāṟ pēcappaṭavillai. āṉāl aṅku atu ilakkiya moḻiyāka iruntuvantatu. pāli, camakkirutac coṟkaḷai ēṟṟukkoṇṭu ilaṅkai makkaḷ vaḻaṅkiya moḻi ciṅkaḷamāka māṟiṟṟu. ki.pi. 10ām nūṟṟāṇṭaḷavil, mutal mutal ciṅkaḷamoḻiyil nūl ceyyappaṭṭatu. ciṅkaḷa moḻiamaipput tamiḻai ottuḷḷatu. itu camakkirutac coṟkaḷ tamiḻuṭaṉ cērntu, ataṉai vēṟupaṭuttiya maikku oru ciṟanta eṭuttukkāṭṭu.
1. The non-Aryan magicians and the Dravidians became Brahmans. South Indian tradition records numerous instances of Brahmans as taking wives from the lower castes. In many cases non-Brahmans were admitted into the Brahmana caste, as sufficient Brahmanas were not available when the reformed Indo-Aryan religion obtained referance. Prehistone ancient and Hindu India P. 38 - R.D. Banerji.

1. By the Pictorial hieroglyphic inscription found and interpreted on the Walls of the temple of the Queen Haslitop at Derel Bahri, we see that this Punt can be no other than India. For many ages the Egyptians traded with their old homes and the reference here made by them to the names of the princes of Punt and its fauna and flora, especially the nomenclature of various precious woods to be found but in India, leaves us scarcely room for the smallest doubt that the old civilization of Egypt is the direct outcome of that of the older India - The Theosophist - March 1881 p. 123 Quoted in “Hindu Superiority” by Har Baeus Sarda.
The ancient Abyssinians as already remarked, were, originally migrators to Africa from the banks of Abuisin a classical name for Indus - Heran’s Historical Researches Vol 2 - p. 310
1. When the hungry swarms of Aryan tribesmen descended upon North-west India, the whole land with the exception of the North East corner was occupied by a long settled Dravidian population, split into many Castes and tribes-
The mythic society journal vol IV p 157 - James Hornwell.
It will appear that the civilization of the Indus valley was associated with the speakers of the Dravidian languages. Lastly the Brahmi script of later Vedic civilization is itself tracad to Indus valley pictographs It will thus appear that the Dravidian speakers were the latest occupants of India before the Indo-Aryan arrived. - Hindu India - Radha Kumud Mukerji - p. 38
1. cātikkalappum uṇṭāyiṟṟu.
There was also inevitably at work a process of fusion between the Aryans and the non-Aryans by inter marriage or by alliance - Hindu Civilization - R.K. Mookerji.
The distinction between the Aryan and non-Aryan then grew less sharp while social and political conditions became more complicated. The children of Dasu concubines in the Aryan households and those of Aryan women captured by Dravidian chieftains adopted Aryan customs and religious rites. The fair women of Aryan descent were an irresistible attraction for Dravidian chieftains and racial pride did not prevent Indo-Aryan families from giving a daughter in marriage to a dark skinned neighbour for a sufficient consideration-But as Dravidian society was matriarchal such inter marriages, with or without consent always exerted powerful influence in the Aryanization of India, for in the course of time all the highest Dravidian families both in the north and south claimed Aryan descent on their mothers’ side and adopted Aryan customs and religion - A Short History of India E.B. Havell. - p.p. 1926,
1. Zeus had two eyes placed naturally and the third upon the forehead. They say that Priam had this bust of Zeus from his ancestor laomedon - The third eye of this ancient statue was in the forehead; and it seems that the Hidus have a symbolical figure of the same kind - The symbolical language of ancient art and mythology - p. 73 R.P. Knight
2. Juno, Venue, Cybele, Rhoea the Syrian goddess, the armed Phallus, lris, Ceres and Anna Perenna.
1. ‘That contact between Vedism and the indigenous religion of the Indus valley modified both (a) by making the cult of yoga acceptable to Vedism, which formerly believed only in sacrifice and (b) by the retention of non-vedic elements in popular religion. As to the cult of yoga, its indigeous origin is seen in certain stone statues discovered at Mohenjodaro showing ascetics with eyes half shut in contemplation and fixed on the tip of the nose. These according to Chanda supply the missing links between the prehistoric Indus valley civilization and latter civilization of ‘India’ - Buddhist, Jain or Brahmanical - Memoir No 41 of arch, survey quoted in Hindu India.
2. It is not generally admitted that a great deal of the ancient and medieval myth and legend enshrined in the Sanskrit epics and puranas is of Non-Aryan origin, and that even in vedic mythology certain pre-Aryan elements are present. Puranic myths of the gods and legends of kings, heroes, and sages, in the form in which we find them in the Sanskrit works, represent undoubtedly a considerable amount of modification from other original forms, whether Aryan or non-Aryan. The non-aryan speaking masses of Northern India became Aryanised in language, and their tales and legends were retold as a matter of course in the Aryan language of their adoption.
- Bulletin of the school of oriental studies Vol. VIII (1935 - 1937) S.K. Chatterjee.
1. The Dravidian substratum in Sanskrit has been discovered by several scholars, and Kittel in his Kannada Dictionary has given some 450 Sanskrit words with possible Dravidian connections. A few other words in both Sanskrit and Prakrit and the vernaculars have subsequently been suggested as being of Dravidian origin by other scholars. Sylvain Levi and his pupils, Jean przyluski of Paris, and Prabodh Chandra Bagchi of Calcutta and to some extent the present writer suggested a number of words in the Indo aryan as being Austric (Kol or Munda) origin and affinity - Non-Aryan Elements in Indo aryan S.K. Chatterji.
- Journal of the Greater India Society Vol. 3
The Dravidian names of things and operations connected with all these arts of peace are native and not foreign (not borrowed from Sanskrit) The question has not yet been investigated, but on enquiry it will most probably turn out that many Sanskrit words connected with these arts were borrowed from Dravidian.
- Age of the Mantras P. 15, P.T.S. Iyengar.
1. As regards the material aspects of non-aryan civilization, the Rigveda refers to towns and forts broad and wide, full of kine, of 100 Pillars, built of stone, to autumnal forts as refuge against inundatons and to 100 Cities in a non-Aryan kingdom Even the Vedic God Indra is designated for the occasion as Purandara - sucker of cities - Hindu India - R.K.M. p-30
“Brahmanical legends refer to the strong and wealthy cities on or near the banks of the Indus of which the Aryans took possession after a hard struggle for their adversaries were well armed, possessed horse and chariots, and built castles of stones. Several of the places afterwards celebrated in Indian History, such as Taksha-sela, Mathura and Ujjin were said to have been founded by these non-Aryan people who were probably Dravidian race and perhaps connected with the ancient Sumerians, the people of southern Babylonia whose history has been traced back to the fourth millennium B, C. These Dravidians called by the Aryans Asuras Eaityas, Dasus or Nagas were mostly a maritime and trading people. In the Mahabharata they described as great magicians. They worshipped the sun and the serpent like people of ancient media, with whom, perhaps they had trade connections. The amalgamation was either through military conquest or by peaceful penetration. Aryan and Non-aryan cultures gradually transformed the simple tribal organisatin of Vedic society into powerful political status and finally made ocean instead of the river, the boundary of Aryavartha. - A short History of India p 26 - E.B. Havell.
1. Dravidian place names are sometimes traced to Mesopotamia and Iran, while an ancient language spoken at Mittani reveals striking similarities to modern Dravidian of India - Hindu India p. 38
2. I am however inclined to agree with late Sir Herbert Risely in his view that the Dravidian peoples are autochthons of the Deccan and the South of the Peninsula. Dr Haddon says: The Dravidian may have been always in India as has already been said. H.R. Hail holds the view that the Sumerians came into Western Asia from South India where even their writing may have been invented which in its later developed form in Babylonia was the Cuniform Character in the inscriptions. Bahui only marks a stage in the track of the Dravidians on their way from India to sumer. The Sumerian civilisation is the earliest known in the world and if its Dravidian origin be accepted then a very ancient date for the Dravidian civilization must have to be admitted. The date of Sargon of Akkad, the founder of the dynasty of Akkad has been fixed at 2800 B.C. Now Akkad got her civilization from Sumer allowing for the development of sumerian Civilization. The Dravidian settlement of Sumer may be probably placed at a date not less than 5000 B.C. The bird and serpent Myth - Prof. Kalipada Mitra. - The O.J.O.T. mythic Society Vol XVI.
1. From the recent discovery of the sumerian and Hittite relics in the Indus valley and elsewhere in India it is becoming more and more clear that India never stood isolated from the rest of Asia in the past. but that her Culture and civilization formed not only an integral part of the ancient chaleolithic culture of Asia and Europe but also one of the oldest and the richest treasure house from which all other civilizations derived their largest contributions to which they gave the best of their products in exchange. From the Indus valley one section of people afterwards came to be designated as the Dravidians, marched towards the west both by sea as well as by land, settled down in the province of Sumer in Lower Babylonia under the name of Sumerians and spread Sumero - Dravidian Civilization as far as the Holy land of Palestine and Jerusalem and thence to Europe and Africa.
- Quarterly journal of the mythic society Vol. XIX No. 3
